========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, yeah… I’m here because I keep getting up when I’m not supposed to. Like at work, I’m supposed to stay at my station, and I’ll just suddenly walk off or pop up like I need to go do something, and sometimes I don’t even really think it through first. It’s like my body moves before my brain catches up.

And it’s not just work — in meetings too, when I’m supposed to stay seated and listen, I’ll stand up or start moving around, and people notice. My supervisor has talked to me about it more than once, and now she’s saying it’s becoming a safety issue, because on the production floor I can’t just leave my spot or take off randomly. I don’t mean to cause problems, but I keep doing it, and it’s starting to get serious.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, definitely. It’s not just like I’m bored or I’m choosing to wander off. It feels like this pressure starts building up in my body, especially if I’m standing still by the machine or sitting through training. Like my legs want to go before I’ve even decided anything.

I’ll be trying to stay where I’m supposed to be, and I can feel myself getting more and more uncomfortable, like I need to move right then — pace, stretch, walk away, go check something, anything. If I don’t move, it feels almost unbearable, like I’m trapped in place. Then I’ll just get up or start shifting around before I even fully think about it.

My supervisor has talked to me about it more than once, and now she’s saying it’s becoming a safety issue, because on the production floor I can’t just leave my spot or take off randomly. Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly, but I do get really uncomfortable and kind of worked up when it builds. Like if I’m trying to stand still by the machine, or if I’m sitting through training and I’m supposed to just stay there and pay attention, it feels like this pressure is rising in my body.

It’s like I have to move right then — pace, stretch, shift around, go do something, anything. And if I try to force myself not to, I start feeling more and more bothered, like I’m trapped in my own skin almost. So yeah, I get upset in the sense that it feels unbearable, and then I just end up moving before I can really talk myself out of it.

I don’t mean to cause problems, but I keep doing it, and it’s starting to get serious.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless or like you need to move when this happens?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not just like I’m bored or I’m choosing to wander off. It feels like this pressure starts building up in my body, especially if I’m standing still by the machine or sitting through training. Like my legs want to go before I’ve even decided anything.\n\nI’ll be trying to stay where I’m supposed to be, and I can feel myself getting more and more uncomfortable, like I need to move right then — pace, stretch, walk away, go check something, anything. If I don’t move, it feels almost unbearable, like I’m trapped in place. Then I’ll just get up or start shifting around before I even fully think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when this pressure to move builds up inside you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly, but I do get really uncomfortable and kind of worked up when it builds. Like if I’m trying to stand still by the machine, or if I’m sitting through training and I’m supposed to just stay there and pay attention, it feels like this pressure is rising in my body.\n\nIt’s like I have to move right then — pace, stretch, shift around, go do something, anything. And if I try to force myself not to, I start feeling more and more bothered, like I’m trapped in my own skin almost. So yeah, I get upset in the sense that it feels unbearable, and then I just end up moving before I can really talk myself out of it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid you experience these feelings of restlessness and needing to move when you were a child?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I did. I mean, looking back, that was kind of always me, even when I was little. In elementary school I was the kid who could not stay in my seat. Like, the teacher would tell everyone to sit down and do their work, and I’d be up sharpening a pencil, walking around, messing with something, or just kind of drifting away from my desk without really thinking about it.

And I talked over the teacher a lot too. Not always trying to be rude, but I’d just blurt things out, answer before I was called on, interrupt because the thought was there and it felt like I had to say it right then. I remember getting notes sent home for that — for talking too much, blurting out, getting out of my seat, wandering around the classroom. It was pretty much a thing people noticed about me even back then.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, very easily. At work especially, on the line, it doesn’t take much at all. Like I can be at my station knowing I’m supposed to keep my eyes on what I’m doing, and then one beep from a different machine will happen, or somebody walks by with a pallet jack, and it’s like my eyes go there before I even choose to look.

And it’s not just my eyes, it’s like my whole brain gets pulled over there with it. I’ll be thinking about that sound or watching where they’re going, and then I have to drag myself back to my own station like, “No, focus on this.” But it happens over and over, even when I know I need to stay locked in. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to ignore my task — it’s just really easy for something else to grab me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do that a lot, honestly. I don’t usually mean it like I’m trying to be rude, but I’ll cut in before I even realize I’m doing it.

Like at work, if a couple coworkers are talking about something and I hear part of it, I’ll just jump in with my thought, even if nobody was really talking to me. Or if the lead is explaining something or asking a question, I’ll answer before they’re even done finishing the sentence, because I think I already know where they’re going with it. Then sometimes I’m wrong, or I’ve talked over them, and everybody kind of looks irritated.

It’s embarrassing because afterward I can tell I did it again, but in the moment it feels like the words just come out before I’ve had time to stop them. I’ve had people be like, “Let me finish,” or “I wasn’t asking you,” and then I feel bad, but it still keeps happening.

Like at work, I’m supposed to stay at my station, and I’ll just suddenly walk off or pop up like I need to go do something, and sometimes I don’t even really think it through first. It’s like my body moves before my brain catches up.\n\nAnd it’s not just work — in meetings too, when I’m supposed to stay seated and listen, I’ll stand up or start moving around, and people notice. My supervisor has talked to me about it more than once, and now she’s saying it’s becoming a safety issue, because on the production floor I can’t just leave my spot or take off randomly. I don’t mean to cause problems, but I keep doing it, and it’s starting to get serious.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless or like you need to move when this happens?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not just like I’m bored or I’m choosing to wander off. It feels like this pressure starts building up in my body, especially if I’m standing still by the machine or sitting through training. Like my legs want to go before I’ve even decided anything.\n\nI’ll be trying to stay where I’m supposed to be, and I can feel myself getting more and more uncomfortable, like I need to move right then — pace, stretch, walk away, go check something, anything. If I don’t move, it feels almost unbearable, like I’m trapped in place. Then I’ll just get up or start shifting around before I even fully think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when this pressure to move builds up inside you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly, but I do get really uncomfortable and kind of worked up when it builds. Like if I’m trying to stand still by the machine, or if I’m sitting through training and I’m supposed to just stay there and pay attention, it feels like this pressure is rising in my body.\n\nIt’s like I have to move right then — pace, stretch, shift around, go do something, anything. And if I try to force myself not to, I start feeling more and more bothered, like I’m trapped in my own skin almost. So yeah, I get upset in the sense that it feels unbearable, and then I just end up moving before I can really talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness and needing to move when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. I mean, looking back, that was kind of always me, even when I was little. In elementary school I was the kid who could not stay in my seat. Like, the teacher would tell everyone to sit down and do their work, and I’d be up sharpening a pencil, walking around, messing with something, or just kind of drifting away from my desk without really thinking about it.\n\nAnd I talked over the teacher a lot too. Not always trying to be rude, but I’d just blurt things out, answer before I was called on, interrupt because the thought was there and it felt like I had to say it right then. I remember getting notes sent home for that — for talking too much, blurting out, getting out of my seat, wandering around the classroom. It was pretty much a thing people noticed about me even back then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted or having trouble focusing on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, very easily. At work especially, on the line, it doesn’t take much at all. Like I can be at my station knowing I’m supposed to keep my eyes on what I’m doing, and then one beep from a different machine will happen, or somebody walks by with a pallet jack, and it’s like my eyes go there before I even choose to look.\n\nAnd it’s not just my eyes, it’s like my whole brain gets pulled over there with it. I’ll be thinking about that sound or watching where they’re going, and then I have to drag myself back to my own station like, “No, focus on this.” But it happens over and over, even when I know I need to stay locked in. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to ignore my task — it’s just really easy for something else to grab me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that a lot, honestly. I don’t usually mean it like I’m trying to be rude, but I’ll cut in before I even realize I’m doing it.\n\nLike at work, if a couple coworkers are talking about something and I hear part of it, I’ll just jump in with my thought, even if nobody was really talking to me. Or if the lead is explaining something or asking a question, I’ll answer before they’re even done finishing the sentence, because I think I already know where they’re going with it. Then sometimes I’m wrong, or I’ve talked over them, and everybody kind of looks irritated.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because afterward I can tell I did it again, but in the moment it feels like the words just come out before I’ve had time to stop them. I’ve had people be like, “Let me finish,” or “I wasn’t asking you,” and then I feel bad, but it still keeps happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yeah, it has. I mean, it affects people around me, but the biggest place it’s been really obvious is at work, because that kind of spills into relationships with coworkers too.

Like, I’ve been written up by supervisors for safety stuff and production problems, and it’s not just a little warning where everyone forgets about it. It feels like it follows me. I’m trying to keep my job, and I’ve been wanting to work toward getting certified, but when I keep getting written up or people see me as someone who causes problems on the line, it makes that feel like it could get taken away from me.

And with coworkers, it’s gotten awkward. During handoffs, people don’t always want to partner with me or depend on me because they’re worried something will get missed or messed up. I can tell they get frustrated, and then I feel embarrassed and kind of on edge, because I know they’re thinking, like, “Great, I have to work with her again.” So yeah, it’s not just inside my head — it’s actually affecting how people deal with me and whether they trust me at work.

If I don’t move, it feels almost unbearable, like I’m trapped in place. Then I’ll just get up or start shifting around before I even fully think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when this pressure to move builds up inside you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly, but I do get really uncomfortable and kind of worked up when it builds. Like if I’m trying to stand still by the machine, or if I’m sitting through training and I’m supposed to just stay there and pay attention, it feels like this pressure is rising in my body.\n\nIt’s like I have to move right then — pace, stretch, shift around, go do something, anything. And if I try to force myself not to, I start feeling more and more bothered, like I’m trapped in my own skin almost. So yeah, I get upset in the sense that it feels unbearable, and then I just end up moving before I can really talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness and needing to move when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. I mean, looking back, that was kind of always me, even when I was little. In elementary school I was the kid who could not stay in my seat. Like, the teacher would tell everyone to sit down and do their work, and I’d be up sharpening a pencil, walking around, messing with something, or just kind of drifting away from my desk without really thinking about it.\n\nAnd I talked over the teacher a lot too. Not always trying to be rude, but I’d just blurt things out, answer before I was called on, interrupt because the thought was there and it felt like I had to say it right then. I remember getting notes sent home for that — for talking too much, blurting out, getting out of my seat, wandering around the classroom. It was pretty much a thing people noticed about me even back then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted or having trouble focusing on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, very easily. At work especially, on the line, it doesn’t take much at all. Like I can be at my station knowing I’m supposed to keep my eyes on what I’m doing, and then one beep from a different machine will happen, or somebody walks by with a pallet jack, and it’s like my eyes go there before I even choose to look.\n\nAnd it’s not just my eyes, it’s like my whole brain gets pulled over there with it. I’ll be thinking about that sound or watching where they’re going, and then I have to drag myself back to my own station like, “No, focus on this.” But it happens over and over, even when I know I need to stay locked in. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to ignore my task — it’s just really easy for something else to grab me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that a lot, honestly. I don’t usually mean it like I’m trying to be rude, but I’ll cut in before I even realize I’m doing it.\n\nLike at work, if a couple coworkers are talking about something and I hear part of it, I’ll just jump in with my thought, even if nobody was really talking to me. Or if the lead is explaining something or asking a question, I’ll answer before they’re even done finishing the sentence, because I think I already know where they’re going with it. Then sometimes I’m wrong, or I’ve talked over them, and everybody kind of looks irritated.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because afterward I can tell I did it again, but in the moment it feels like the words just come out before I’ve had time to stop them. I’ve had people be like, “Let me finish,” or “I wasn’t asking you,” and then I feel bad, but it still keeps happening.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms caused problems in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I mean, it affects people around me, but the biggest place it’s been really obvious is at work, because that kind of spills into relationships with coworkers too.\n\nLike, I’ve been written up by supervisors for safety stuff and production problems, and it’s not just a little warning where everyone forgets about it. It feels like it follows me. I’m trying to keep my job, and I’ve been wanting to work toward getting certified, but when I keep getting written up or people see me as someone who causes problems on the line, it makes that feel like it could get taken away from me.\n\nAnd with coworkers, it’s gotten awkward. During handoffs, people don’t always want to partner with me or depend on me because they’re worried something will get missed or messed up. I can tell they get frustrated, and then I feel embarrassed and kind of on edge, because I know they’re thinking, like, “Great, I have to work with her again.” So yeah, it’s not just inside my head — it’s actually affecting how people deal with me and whether they trust me at work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Yeah… that’s actually one of the main reasons I came in. I keep doing this thing where I just leave my station before I’ve really thought it through. Like I’m supposed to stay put on the line, and something will happen or I’ll feel like I need to check something, and I’m already walking away before I’ve even stopped to think, “Am I allowed to leave right now?” or “Is this safe?”

It happens in meetings too. If we’re supposed to be sitting there listening, I’ll pop up or move around like I’m going to go do something, and then I realize people are looking at me because I wasn’t supposed to get up. It’s not like I plan it out. It’s more like my body starts moving first, and then my brain catches up after.

My supervisor has been on me about it because on the floor it can become a safety issue. Like if I step away from my station at the wrong time or just take off without letting someone know, it can mess with the process or put me where I shouldn’t be. And I hate that, because I’m not trying to be careless, but I do act before I think a lot. That “just go” feeling happens really fast.

My supervisor has talked to me about it more than once, and now she’s saying it’s becoming a safety issue, because on the production floor I can’t just leave my spot or take off randomly. I don’t mean to cause problems, but I keep doing it, and it’s starting to get serious.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless or like you need to move when this happens?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not just like I’m bored or I’m choosing to wander off. It feels like this pressure starts building up in my body, especially if I’m standing still by the machine or sitting through training. Like my legs want to go before I’ve even decided anything.\n\nI’ll be trying to stay where I’m supposed to be, and I can feel myself getting more and more uncomfortable, like I need to move right then — pace, stretch, walk away, go check something, anything. If I don’t move, it feels almost unbearable, like I’m trapped in place. Then I’ll just get up or start shifting around before I even fully think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when this pressure to move builds up inside you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly, but I do get really uncomfortable and kind of worked up when it builds. Like if I’m trying to stand still by the machine, or if I’m sitting through training and I’m supposed to just stay there and pay attention, it feels like this pressure is rising in my body.\n\nIt’s like I have to move right then — pace, stretch, shift around, go do something, anything. And if I try to force myself not to, I start feeling more and more bothered, like I’m trapped in my own skin almost. So yeah, I get upset in the sense that it feels unbearable, and then I just end up moving before I can really talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness and needing to move when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. I mean, looking back, that was kind of always me, even when I was little. In elementary school I was the kid who could not stay in my seat. Like, the teacher would tell everyone to sit down and do their work, and I’d be up sharpening a pencil, walking around, messing with something, or just kind of drifting away from my desk without really thinking about it.\n\nAnd I talked over the teacher a lot too. Not always trying to be rude, but I’d just blurt things out, answer before I was called on, interrupt because the thought was there and it felt like I had to say it right then. I remember getting notes sent home for that — for talking too much, blurting out, getting out of my seat, wandering around the classroom. It was pretty much a thing people noticed about me even back then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted or having trouble focusing on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, very easily. At work especially, on the line, it doesn’t take much at all. Like I can be at my station knowing I’m supposed to keep my eyes on what I’m doing, and then one beep from a different machine will happen, or somebody walks by with a pallet jack, and it’s like my eyes go there before I even choose to look.\n\nAnd it’s not just my eyes, it’s like my whole brain gets pulled over there with it. I’ll be thinking about that sound or watching where they’re going, and then I have to drag myself back to my own station like, “No, focus on this.” But it happens over and over, even when I know I need to stay locked in. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to ignore my task — it’s just really easy for something else to grab me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that a lot, honestly. I don’t usually mean it like I’m trying to be rude, but I’ll cut in before I even realize I’m doing it.\n\nLike at work, if a couple coworkers are talking about something and I hear part of it, I’ll just jump in with my thought, even if nobody was really talking to me. Or if the lead is explaining something or asking a question, I’ll answer before they’re even done finishing the sentence, because I think I already know where they’re going with it. Then sometimes I’m wrong, or I’ve talked over them, and everybody kind of looks irritated.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because afterward I can tell I did it again, but in the moment it feels like the words just come out before I’ve had time to stop them. I’ve had people be like, “Let me finish,” or “I wasn’t asking you,” and then I feel bad, but it still keeps happening.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms caused problems in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I mean, it affects people around me, but the biggest place it’s been really obvious is at work, because that kind of spills into relationships with coworkers too.\n\nLike, I’ve been written up by supervisors for safety stuff and production problems, and it’s not just a little warning where everyone forgets about it. It feels like it follows me. I’m trying to keep my job, and I’ve been wanting to work toward getting certified, but when I keep getting written up or people see me as someone who causes problems on the line, it makes that feel like it could get taken away from me.\n\nAnd with coworkers, it’s gotten awkward. During handoffs, people don’t always want to partner with me or depend on me because they’re worried something will get missed or messed up. I can tell they get frustrated, and then I feel embarrassed and kind of on edge, because I know they’re thinking, like, “Great, I have to work with her again.” So yeah, it’s not just inside my head — it’s actually affecting how people deal with me and whether they trust me at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking, like grabbing something or doing something physical without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually one of the main reasons I came in. I keep doing this thing where I just leave my station before I’ve really thought it through. Like I’m supposed to stay put on the line, and something will happen or I’ll feel like I need to check something, and I’m already walking away before I’ve even stopped to think, “Am I allowed to leave right now?” or “Is this safe?”\n\nIt happens in meetings too. If we’re supposed to be sitting there listening, I’ll pop up or move around like I’m going to go do something, and then I realize people are looking at me because I wasn’t supposed to get up. It’s not like I plan it out. It’s more like my body starts moving first, and then my brain catches up after.\n\nMy supervisor has been on me about it because on the floor it can become a safety issue. Like if I step away from my station at the wrong time or just take off without letting someone know, it can mess with the process or put me where I shouldn’t be. And I hate that, because I’m not trying to be careless, but I do act before I think a lot. That “just go” feeling happens really fast.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F90.0", "F90.8"], "note": "", "is_final": false}
=====================================

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m honestly not totally sure how much it’s affected family or friends, like not in a big obvious way that I can point to the same way I can with work. I mean, I’m sure I probably annoy people sometimes or talk too fast or jump into things, but nobody in my family has sat me down like, “This is ruining our relationship,” or anything like that.

With friends, I don’t know… I don’t have a clear example where I can say, yes, this caused a fight or ended a friendship. Sometimes I worry I’m a lot to deal with, but that might just be me overthinking it. So I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed major problems there, or at least not ones people have directly told me about.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I’m here because I keep getting up when I’m not supposed to. Like at work, I’m supposed to stay at my station, and I’ll just suddenly walk off or pop up like I need to go do something, and sometimes I don’t even really think it through first. It’s like my body moves before my brain catches up.\n\nAnd it’s not just work — in meetings too, when I’m supposed to stay seated and listen, I’ll stand up or start moving around, and people notice. My supervisor has talked to me about it more than once, and now she’s saying it’s becoming a safety issue, because on the production floor I can’t just leave my spot or take off randomly. I don’t mean to cause problems, but I keep doing it, and it’s starting to get serious.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless or like you need to move when this happens?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not just like I’m bored or I’m choosing to wander off. It feels like this pressure starts building up in my body, especially if I’m standing still by the machine or sitting through training. Like my legs want to go before I’ve even decided anything.\n\nI’ll be trying to stay where I’m supposed to be, and I can feel myself getting more and more uncomfortable, like I need to move right then — pace, stretch, walk away, go check something, anything. If I don’t move, it feels almost unbearable, like I’m trapped in place. Then I’ll just get up or start shifting around before I even fully think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when this pressure to move builds up inside you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly, but I do get really uncomfortable and kind of worked up when it builds. Like if I’m trying to stand still by the machine, or if I’m sitting through training and I’m supposed to just stay there and pay attention, it feels like this pressure is rising in my body.\n\nIt’s like I have to move right then — pace, stretch, shift around, go do something, anything. And if I try to force myself not to, I start feeling more and more bothered, like I’m trapped in my own skin almost. So yeah, I get upset in the sense that it feels unbearable, and then I just end up moving before I can really talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness and needing to move when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. I mean, looking back, that was kind of always me, even when I was little. In elementary school I was the kid who could not stay in my seat. Like, the teacher would tell everyone to sit down and do their work, and I’d be up sharpening a pencil, walking around, messing with something, or just kind of drifting away from my desk without really thinking about it.\n\nAnd I talked over the teacher a lot too. Not always trying to be rude, but I’d just blurt things out, answer before I was called on, interrupt because the thought was there and it felt like I had to say it right then. I remember getting notes sent home for that — for talking too much, blurting out, getting out of my seat, wandering around the classroom. It was pretty much a thing people noticed about me even back then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted or having trouble focusing on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, very easily. At work especially, on the line, it doesn’t take much at all. Like I can be at my station knowing I’m supposed to keep my eyes on what I’m doing, and then one beep from a different machine will happen, or somebody walks by with a pallet jack, and it’s like my eyes go there before I even choose to look.\n\nAnd it’s not just my eyes, it’s like my whole brain gets pulled over there with it. I’ll be thinking about that sound or watching where they’re going, and then I have to drag myself back to my own station like, “No, focus on this.” But it happens over and over, even when I know I need to stay locked in. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to ignore my task — it’s just really easy for something else to grab me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that a lot, honestly. I don’t usually mean it like I’m trying to be rude, but I’ll cut in before I even realize I’m doing it.\n\nLike at work, if a couple coworkers are talking about something and I hear part of it, I’ll just jump in with my thought, even if nobody was really talking to me. Or if the lead is explaining something or asking a question, I’ll answer before they’re even done finishing the sentence, because I think I already know where they’re going with it. Then sometimes I’m wrong, or I’ve talked over them, and everybody kind of looks irritated.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because afterward I can tell I did it again, but in the moment it feels like the words just come out before I’ve had time to stop them. I’ve had people be like, “Let me finish,” or “I wasn’t asking you,” and then I feel bad, but it still keeps happening.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms caused problems in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I mean, it affects people around me, but the biggest place it’s been really obvious is at work, because that kind of spills into relationships with coworkers too.\n\nLike, I’ve been written up by supervisors for safety stuff and production problems, and it’s not just a little warning where everyone forgets about it. It feels like it follows me. I’m trying to keep my job, and I’ve been wanting to work toward getting certified, but when I keep getting written up or people see me as someone who causes problems on the line, it makes that feel like it could get taken away from me.\n\nAnd with coworkers, it’s gotten awkward. During handoffs, people don’t always want to partner with me or depend on me because they’re worried something will get missed or messed up. I can tell they get frustrated, and then I feel embarrassed and kind of on edge, because I know they’re thinking, like, “Great, I have to work with her again.” So yeah, it’s not just inside my head — it’s actually affecting how people deal with me and whether they trust me at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking, like grabbing something or doing something physical without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually one of the main reasons I came in. I keep doing this thing where I just leave my station before I’ve really thought it through. Like I’m supposed to stay put on the line, and something will happen or I’ll feel like I need to check something, and I’m already walking away before I’ve even stopped to think, “Am I allowed to leave right now?” or “Is this safe?”\n\nIt happens in meetings too. If we’re supposed to be sitting there listening, I’ll pop up or move around like I’m going to go do something, and then I realize people are looking at me because I wasn’t supposed to get up. It’s not like I plan it out. It’s more like my body starts moving first, and then my brain catches up after.\n\nMy supervisor has been on me about it because on the floor it can become a safety issue. Like if I step away from my station at the wrong time or just take off without letting someone know, it can mess with the process or put me where I shouldn’t be. And I hate that, because I’m not trying to be careless, but I do act before I think a lot. That “just go” feeling happens really fast.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms causing problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not totally sure how much it’s affected family or friends, like not in a big obvious way that I can point to the same way I can with work. I mean, I’m sure I probably annoy people sometimes or talk too fast or jump into things, but nobody in my family has sat me down like, “This is ruining our relationship,” or anything like that.\n\nWith friends, I don’t know… I don’t have a clear example where I can say, yes, this caused a fight or ended a friendship. Sometimes I worry I’m a lot to deal with, but that might just be me overthinking it. So I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed major problems there, or at least not ones people have directly told me about.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "patient_turn_index: 10\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."

{"key":"duration","name":"Duration","description":"1 year and 2 months","reason":"The doctor is asking about how long the restlessness and impulsivity have been present and whether their intensity has changed over time."}

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Predominantly Hyperactive/Impulsive Presentation), in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have your symptoms caused problems in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I mean, it affects people around me, but the biggest place it’s been really obvious is at work, because that kind of spills into relationships with coworkers too.\n\nLike, I’ve been written up by supervisors for safety stuff and production problems, and it’s not just a little warning where everyone forgets about it. It feels like it follows me. I’m trying to keep my job, and I’ve been wanting to work toward getting certified, but when I keep getting written up or people see me as someone who causes problems on the line, it makes that feel like it could get taken away from me.\n\nAnd with coworkers, it’s gotten awkward. During handoffs, people don’t always want to partner with me or depend on me because they’re worried something will get missed or messed up. I can tell they get frustrated, and then I feel embarrassed and kind of on edge, because I know they’re thinking, like, “Great, I have to work with her again.” So yeah, it’s not just inside my head — it’s actually affecting how people deal with me and whether they trust me at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking, like grabbing something or doing something physical without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually one of the main reasons I came in. I keep doing this thing where I just leave my station before I’ve really thought it through. Like I’m supposed to stay put on the line, and something will happen or I’ll feel like I need to check something, and I’m already walking away before I’ve even stopped to think, “Am I allowed to leave right now?” or “Is this safe?”\n\nIt happens in meetings too. If we’re supposed to be sitting there listening, I’ll pop up or move around like I’m going to go do something, and then I realize people are looking at me because I wasn’t supposed to get up. It’s not like I plan it out. It’s more like my body starts moving first, and then my brain catches up after.\n\nMy supervisor has been on me about it because on the floor it can become a safety issue. Like if I step away from my station at the wrong time or just take off without letting someone know, it can mess with the process or put me where I shouldn’t be. And I hate that, because I’m not trying to be careless, but I do act before I think a lot. That “just go” feeling happens really fast.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms causing problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not totally sure how much it’s affected family or friends, like not in a big obvious way that I can point to the same way I can with work. I mean, I’m sure I probably annoy people sometimes or talk too fast or jump into things, but nobody in my family has sat me down like, “This is ruining our relationship,” or anything like that.\n\nWith friends, I don’t know… I don’t have a clear example where I can say, yes, this caused a fight or ended a friendship. Sometimes I worry I’m a lot to deal with, but that might just be me overthinking it. So I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed major problems there, or at least not ones people have directly told me about.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and impulsivity consistently over the years, or have they varied in intensity?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / production_worker / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been causing serious problems for about 1 year and 2 months, ever since I tried to tighten up my habits and move toward forklift and CNC certification.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s been more like… it’s gotten seriously noticeable over the last year and a little bit. I’d say about a year and two months, give or take. That’s when it really started causing problems, not just being something I could brush off.

It kind of lined up with when I was trying to tighten up my habits at work and actually move forward, like working toward forklift and CNC certification. I was trying to be more serious and prove I could handle more responsibility, and that’s when these things started standing out more because I couldn’t just wing it anymore. Like before, maybe I was restless or impulsive and just thought, “That’s how I am,” but once I was trying to be consistent and follow everything exactly, it became a real issue.

So I don’t know if it was there before and I just didn’t pay as much attention, but the serious problems from it have been going on for about a year and two months.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I’m here because I keep getting up when I’m not supposed to. Like at work, I’m supposed to stay at my station, and I’ll just suddenly walk off or pop up like I need to go do something, and sometimes I don’t even really think it through first. It’s like my body moves before my brain catches up.\n\nAnd it’s not just work — in meetings too, when I’m supposed to stay seated and listen, I’ll stand up or start moving around, and people notice. My supervisor has talked to me about it more than once, and now she’s saying it’s becoming a safety issue, because on the production floor I can’t just leave my spot or take off randomly. I don’t mean to cause problems, but I keep doing it, and it’s starting to get serious.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless or like you need to move when this happens?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not just like I’m bored or I’m choosing to wander off. It feels like this pressure starts building up in my body, especially if I’m standing still by the machine or sitting through training. Like my legs want to go before I’ve even decided anything.\n\nI’ll be trying to stay where I’m supposed to be, and I can feel myself getting more and more uncomfortable, like I need to move right then — pace, stretch, walk away, go check something, anything. If I don’t move, it feels almost unbearable, like I’m trapped in place. Then I’ll just get up or start shifting around before I even fully think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when this pressure to move builds up inside you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly, but I do get really uncomfortable and kind of worked up when it builds. Like if I’m trying to stand still by the machine, or if I’m sitting through training and I’m supposed to just stay there and pay attention, it feels like this pressure is rising in my body.\n\nIt’s like I have to move right then — pace, stretch, shift around, go do something, anything. And if I try to force myself not to, I start feeling more and more bothered, like I’m trapped in my own skin almost. So yeah, I get upset in the sense that it feels unbearable, and then I just end up moving before I can really talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness and needing to move when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. I mean, looking back, that was kind of always me, even when I was little. In elementary school I was the kid who could not stay in my seat. Like, the teacher would tell everyone to sit down and do their work, and I’d be up sharpening a pencil, walking around, messing with something, or just kind of drifting away from my desk without really thinking about it.\n\nAnd I talked over the teacher a lot too. Not always trying to be rude, but I’d just blurt things out, answer before I was called on, interrupt because the thought was there and it felt like I had to say it right then. I remember getting notes sent home for that — for talking too much, blurting out, getting out of my seat, wandering around the classroom. It was pretty much a thing people noticed about me even back then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted or having trouble focusing on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, very easily. At work especially, on the line, it doesn’t take much at all. Like I can be at my station knowing I’m supposed to keep my eyes on what I’m doing, and then one beep from a different machine will happen, or somebody walks by with a pallet jack, and it’s like my eyes go there before I even choose to look.\n\nAnd it’s not just my eyes, it’s like my whole brain gets pulled over there with it. I’ll be thinking about that sound or watching where they’re going, and then I have to drag myself back to my own station like, “No, focus on this.” But it happens over and over, even when I know I need to stay locked in. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to ignore my task — it’s just really easy for something else to grab me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that a lot, honestly. I don’t usually mean it like I’m trying to be rude, but I’ll cut in before I even realize I’m doing it.\n\nLike at work, if a couple coworkers are talking about something and I hear part of it, I’ll just jump in with my thought, even if nobody was really talking to me. Or if the lead is explaining something or asking a question, I’ll answer before they’re even done finishing the sentence, because I think I already know where they’re going with it. Then sometimes I’m wrong, or I’ve talked over them, and everybody kind of looks irritated.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because afterward I can tell I did it again, but in the moment it feels like the words just come out before I’ve had time to stop them. I’ve had people be like, “Let me finish,” or “I wasn’t asking you,” and then I feel bad, but it still keeps happening.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms caused problems in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I mean, it affects people around me, but the biggest place it’s been really obvious is at work, because that kind of spills into relationships with coworkers too.\n\nLike, I’ve been written up by supervisors for safety stuff and production problems, and it’s not just a little warning where everyone forgets about it. It feels like it follows me. I’m trying to keep my job, and I’ve been wanting to work toward getting certified, but when I keep getting written up or people see me as someone who causes problems on the line, it makes that feel like it could get taken away from me.\n\nAnd with coworkers, it’s gotten awkward. During handoffs, people don’t always want to partner with me or depend on me because they’re worried something will get missed or messed up. I can tell they get frustrated, and then I feel embarrassed and kind of on edge, because I know they’re thinking, like, “Great, I have to work with her again.” So yeah, it’s not just inside my head — it’s actually affecting how people deal with me and whether they trust me at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking, like grabbing something or doing something physical without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually one of the main reasons I came in. I keep doing this thing where I just leave my station before I’ve really thought it through. Like I’m supposed to stay put on the line, and something will happen or I’ll feel like I need to check something, and I’m already walking away before I’ve even stopped to think, “Am I allowed to leave right now?” or “Is this safe?”\n\nIt happens in meetings too. If we’re supposed to be sitting there listening, I’ll pop up or move around like I’m going to go do something, and then I realize people are looking at me because I wasn’t supposed to get up. It’s not like I plan it out. It’s more like my body starts moving first, and then my brain catches up after.\n\nMy supervisor has been on me about it because on the floor it can become a safety issue. Like if I step away from my station at the wrong time or just take off without letting someone know, it can mess with the process or put me where I shouldn’t be. And I hate that, because I’m not trying to be careless, but I do act before I think a lot. That “just go” feeling happens really fast.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms causing problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not totally sure how much it’s affected family or friends, like not in a big obvious way that I can point to the same way I can with work. I mean, I’m sure I probably annoy people sometimes or talk too fast or jump into things, but nobody in my family has sat me down like, “This is ruining our relationship,” or anything like that.\n\nWith friends, I don’t know… I don’t have a clear example where I can say, yes, this caused a fight or ended a friendship. Sometimes I worry I’m a lot to deal with, but that might just be me overthinking it. So I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed major problems there, or at least not ones people have directly told me about.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and impulsivity consistently over the years, or have they varied in intensity?\n\nPatient: It’s been more like… it’s gotten seriously noticeable over the last year and a little bit. I’d say about a year and two months, give or take. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I’d say about a year and two months, give or take. Like before, maybe I was restless or impulsive and just thought, “That’s how I am,” but once I was trying to be consistent and follow everything exactly, it became a real issue.\n\nSo I don’t know if it was there before and I just didn’t pay as much attention, but the serious problems from it have been going on for about a year and two months.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced these symptoms consistently since childhood?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean… thinking back, yes. It wasn’t just something that started as an adult. Even in elementary school I was kind of known as the kid who couldn’t just sit still and stay in my seat like everybody else.

I remember getting in trouble for talking over the teacher, like blurting stuff out before I was supposed to, or answering when it wasn’t my turn. And I’d get notes sent home because I’d be wandering around the classroom or getting up when I wasn’t supposed to. It wasn’t like I was trying to be bad, at least not how I remember it. I just felt like I had to move or say the thing right then, and then afterward I’d realize I was in trouble again.

So yeah, it’s been there since I was little. It just looks more serious now because the consequences are bigger.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I’m here because I keep getting up when I’m not supposed to. Like at work, I’m supposed to stay at my station, and I’ll just suddenly walk off or pop up like I need to go do something, and sometimes I don’t even really think it through first. It’s like my body moves before my brain catches up.\n\nAnd it’s not just work — in meetings too, when I’m supposed to stay seated and listen, I’ll stand up or start moving around, and people notice. My supervisor has talked to me about it more than once, and now she’s saying it’s becoming a safety issue, because on the production floor I can’t just leave my spot or take off randomly. I don’t mean to cause problems, but I keep doing it, and it’s starting to get serious.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless or like you need to move when this happens?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not just like I’m bored or I’m choosing to wander off. It feels like this pressure starts building up in my body, especially if I’m standing still by the machine or sitting through training. Like my legs want to go before I’ve even decided anything.\n\nI’ll be trying to stay where I’m supposed to be, and I can feel myself getting more and more uncomfortable, like I need to move right then — pace, stretch, walk away, go check something, anything. If I don’t move, it feels almost unbearable, like I’m trapped in place. Then I’ll just get up or start shifting around before I even fully think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when this pressure to move builds up inside you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly, but I do get really uncomfortable and kind of worked up when it builds. Like if I’m trying to stand still by the machine, or if I’m sitting through training and I’m supposed to just stay there and pay attention, it feels like this pressure is rising in my body.\n\nIt’s like I have to move right then — pace, stretch, shift around, go do something, anything. And if I try to force myself not to, I start feeling more and more bothered, like I’m trapped in my own skin almost. So yeah, I get upset in the sense that it feels unbearable, and then I just end up moving before I can really talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness and needing to move when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. I mean, looking back, that was kind of always me, even when I was little. In elementary school I was the kid who could not stay in my seat. Like, the teacher would tell everyone to sit down and do their work, and I’d be up sharpening a pencil, walking around, messing with something, or just kind of drifting away from my desk without really thinking about it.\n\nAnd I talked over the teacher a lot too. Not always trying to be rude, but I’d just blurt things out, answer before I was called on, interrupt because the thought was there and it felt like I had to say it right then. I remember getting notes sent home for that — for talking too much, blurting out, getting out of my seat, wandering around the classroom. It was pretty much a thing people noticed about me even back then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted or having trouble focusing on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, very easily. At work especially, on the line, it doesn’t take much at all. Like I can be at my station knowing I’m supposed to keep my eyes on what I’m doing, and then one beep from a different machine will happen, or somebody walks by with a pallet jack, and it’s like my eyes go there before I even choose to look.\n\nAnd it’s not just my eyes, it’s like my whole brain gets pulled over there with it. I’ll be thinking about that sound or watching where they’re going, and then I have to drag myself back to my own station like, “No, focus on this.” But it happens over and over, even when I know I need to stay locked in. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to ignore my task — it’s just really easy for something else to grab me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that a lot, honestly. I don’t usually mean it like I’m trying to be rude, but I’ll cut in before I even realize I’m doing it.\n\nLike at work, if a couple coworkers are talking about something and I hear part of it, I’ll just jump in with my thought, even if nobody was really talking to me. Or if the lead is explaining something or asking a question, I’ll answer before they’re even done finishing the sentence, because I think I already know where they’re going with it. Then sometimes I’m wrong, or I’ve talked over them, and everybody kind of looks irritated.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because afterward I can tell I did it again, but in the moment it feels like the words just come out before I’ve had time to stop them. I’ve had people be like, “Let me finish,” or “I wasn’t asking you,” and then I feel bad, but it still keeps happening.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms caused problems in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I mean, it affects people around me, but the biggest place it’s been really obvious is at work, because that kind of spills into relationships with coworkers too.\n\nLike, I’ve been written up by supervisors for safety stuff and production problems, and it’s not just a little warning where everyone forgets about it. It feels like it follows me. I’m trying to keep my job, and I’ve been wanting to work toward getting certified, but when I keep getting written up or people see me as someone who causes problems on the line, it makes that feel like it could get taken away from me.\n\nAnd with coworkers, it’s gotten awkward. During handoffs, people don’t always want to partner with me or depend on me because they’re worried something will get missed or messed up. I can tell they get frustrated, and then I feel embarrassed and kind of on edge, because I know they’re thinking, like, “Great, I have to work with her again.” So yeah, it’s not just inside my head — it’s actually affecting how people deal with me and whether they trust me at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking, like grabbing something or doing something physical without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually one of the main reasons I came in. I keep doing this thing where I just leave my station before I’ve really thought it through. Like I’m supposed to stay put on the line, and something will happen or I’ll feel like I need to check something, and I’m already walking away before I’ve even stopped to think, “Am I allowed to leave right now?” or “Is this safe?”\n\nIt happens in meetings too. If we’re supposed to be sitting there listening, I’ll pop up or move around like I’m going to go do something, and then I realize people are looking at me because I wasn’t supposed to get up. It’s not like I plan it out. It’s more like my body starts moving first, and then my brain catches up after.\n\nMy supervisor has been on me about it because on the floor it can become a safety issue. Like if I step away from my station at the wrong time or just take off without letting someone know, it can mess with the process or put me where I shouldn’t be. That “just go” feeling happens really fast.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms causing problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not totally sure how much it’s affected family or friends, like not in a big obvious way that I can point to the same way I can with work. Sometimes I worry I’m a lot to deal with, but that might just be me overthinking it. I’d say about a year and two months, give or take. That’s when it really started causing problems, not just being something I could brush off.\n\nIt kind of lined up with when I was trying to tighten up my habits at work and actually move forward, like working toward forklift and CNC certification. It wasn’t just something that started as an adult. Even in elementary school I was kind of known as the kid who couldn’t just sit still and stay in my seat like everybody else.\n\nI remember getting in trouble for talking over the teacher, like blurting stuff out before I was supposed to, or answering when it wasn’t my turn. Like I can be at my station knowing I’m supposed to keep my eyes on what I’m doing, and then one beep from a different machine will happen, or somebody walks by with a pallet jack, and it’s like my eyes go there before I even choose to look.\n\nAnd it’s not just my eyes, it’s like my whole brain gets pulled over there with it. I’ll be thinking about that sound or watching where they’re going, and then I have to drag myself back to my own station like, “No, focus on this.” But it happens over and over, even when I know I need to stay locked in. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to ignore my task — it’s just really easy for something else to grab me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that a lot, honestly. I don’t usually mean it like I’m trying to be rude, but I’ll cut in before I even realize I’m doing it.\n\nLike at work, if a couple coworkers are talking about something and I hear part of it, I’ll just jump in with my thought, even if nobody was really talking to me. Or if the lead is explaining something or asking a question, I’ll answer before they’re even done finishing the sentence, because I think I already know where they’re going with it. Then sometimes I’m wrong, or I’ve talked over them, and everybody kind of looks irritated.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because afterward I can tell I did it again, but in the moment it feels like the words just come out before I’ve had time to stop them. I’ve had people be like, “Let me finish,” or “I wasn’t asking you,” and then I feel bad, but it still keeps happening.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms caused problems in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I mean, it affects people around me, but the biggest place it’s been really obvious is at work, because that kind of spills into relationships with coworkers too.\n\nLike, I’ve been written up by supervisors for safety stuff and production problems, and it’s not just a little warning where everyone forgets about it. It feels like it follows me. I’m trying to keep my job, and I’ve been wanting to work toward getting certified, but when I keep getting written up or people see me as someone who causes problems on the line, it makes that feel like it could get taken away from me.\n\nAnd with coworkers, it’s gotten awkward. During handoffs, people don’t always want to partner with me or depend on me because they’re worried something will get missed or messed up. I can tell they get frustrated, and then I feel embarrassed and kind of on edge, because I know they’re thinking, like, “Great, I have to work with her again.” So yeah, it’s not just inside my head — it’s actually affecting how people deal with me and whether they trust me at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking, like grabbing something or doing something physical without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually one of the main reasons I came in. I keep doing this thing where I just leave my station before I’ve really thought it through. Like I’m supposed to stay put on the line, and something will happen or I’ll feel like I need to check something, and I’m already walking away before I’ve even stopped to think, “Am I allowed to leave right now?” or “Is this safe?”\n\nIt happens in meetings too. If we’re supposed to be sitting there listening, I’ll pop up or move around like I’m going to go do something, and then I realize people are looking at me because I wasn’t supposed to get up. It’s not like I plan it out. It’s more like my body starts moving first, and then my brain catches up after.\n\nMy supervisor has been on me about it because on the floor it can become a safety issue. Like if I step away from my station at the wrong time or just take off without letting someone know, it can mess with the process or put me where I shouldn’t be. And I hate that, because I’m not trying to be careless, but I do act before I think a lot. That “just go” feeling happens really fast.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms causing problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not totally sure how much it’s affected family or friends, like not in a big obvious way that I can point to the same way I can with work. I mean, I’m sure I probably annoy people sometimes or talk too fast or jump into things, but nobody in my family has sat me down like, “This is ruining our relationship,” or anything like that.\n\nWith friends, I don’t know… I don’t have a clear example where I can say, yes, this caused a fight or ended a friendship. Sometimes I worry I’m a lot to deal with, but that might just be me overthinking it. So I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed major problems there, or at least not ones people have directly told me about.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and impulsivity consistently over the years, or have they varied in intensity?\n\nPatient: It’s been more like… it’s gotten seriously noticeable over the last year and a little bit. I’d say about a year and two months, give or take. That’s when it really started causing problems, not just being something I could brush off.\n\nIt kind of lined up with when I was trying to tighten up my habits at work and actually move forward, like working toward forklift and CNC certification. I was trying to be more serious and prove I could handle more responsibility, and that’s when these things started standing out more because I couldn’t just wing it anymore. Like before, maybe I was restless or impulsive and just thought, “That’s how I am,” but once I was trying to be consistent and follow everything exactly, it became a real issue.\n\nSo I don’t know if it was there before and I just didn’t pay as much attention, but the serious problems from it have been going on for about a year and two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms consistently since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… thinking back, yes. It wasn’t just something that started as an adult. Even in elementary school I was kind of known as the kid who couldn’t just sit still and stay in my seat like everybody else.\n\nI remember getting in trouble for talking over the teacher, like blurting stuff out before I was supposed to, or answering when it wasn’t my turn. And I’d get notes sent home because I’d be wandering around the classroom or getting up when I wasn’t supposed to. It wasn’t like I was trying to be bad, at least not how I remember it. I just felt like I had to move or say the thing right then, and then afterward I’d realize I was in trouble again.\n\nSo yeah, it’s been there since I was little. It just looks more serious now because the consequences are bigger.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

As an adult, the biggest place it’s caused real problems is work. That’s where it’s not just embarrassing or annoying, it’s actually putting things at risk for me.
